Inexistence
1

Abstract




Abstract
.
Equivalences under the affine group

{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of constant Hessian rank
1
surfaces
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
sometimes called
parabolic
, were, among other
objects, studied by
Doubrov, Komrakov, Rabinovich,
Eastwood, Ezhov, Olver, Chen, Merker, Arnaldsson, Valiquette.
Especially, homogeneous models and algebras of differential
invariants in various branches have been fully understood.

Then what about higher dimensions?
We consider hypersurfaces

H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
graphed as
\big\{u=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\big\}
whose Hessian matrix
\big(F_{x_{i}x_{j}}\big)
,
a relative affine invariant, is, similarly, of constant rank
1
.

Are there homogeneous models?

Complete explorations were done by the author on a computer in
dimensions
n=2,3,4,5,6,7
. The first, expected outcome,
was to obtain a complete
classification of homogeneous models in dimensions

n=2,3,4
(forthcoming article, case
n=2
already known).
The second, unexpected outcome, was that in dimensions
n=5,6,7
,

there are no affinely homogenous models!

(Except those that are affinely equivalent to
a product of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with a
homogeneous model in dimensions
2,3,4
.)

The present article establishes such a non-existence result
in every dimension
n\geqslant 5
,
based on the production of a normal form for

\big\{u=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\big\}
,
under
{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
up to order
\leqslant n+5
,
valid in any dimension
n\geqslant 2
.

1
.
Introduction

Let
n\geqslant 1
, let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

u\in\mathbb{R}
and
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
v\in\mathbb{R}
.
We consider equivalences of local analytic hypersurfaces

H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}_{x,u}^{n+1}
and
K^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}_{y,v}^{n+1}

graphed as:

u\,=\,F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,G(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),

under
affine transformations

\Psi\colon\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} y_{1}&\,=\,a_{1,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+a_{1,n}\,x_{n}+b_{1}\,u+\tau_{1},\\
\cdots&\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdot\cdot\\
y_{n}&\,=\,a_{n,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+a_{n,n}\,x_{n}+b_{n}\,u+\tau_{n},\\
v&\,=\,c_{1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+c_{n}\,x_{n}+d\,u+\tau_{0},\end{aligned}

(1.1)

where the
(n+1)\times(n+1)
linear-part

\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)

matrix is invertible,
i.e.
belongs to

{\sf GL}(n+1,\mathbb{R})
.
The collection of all these
transformations is the Lie transformation group

{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{aff}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
of
{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})

consists of the
vector fields:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\,T_{1}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\cdots+T_{n}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}+T_{0}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}\,+

\displaystyle+\Big(A_{1,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{1,n}\,x_{n}+B_{1}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\,+

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdot\,+

\displaystyle+\Big(A_{n,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{n,n}\,x_{n}+B_{n}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\,+

\displaystyle+\Big(C_{1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+C_{n}\,x_{n}+D\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}.

A fixed hypersurface
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
has affine
symmetry group the

local Lie group
—  for
background,
see

[
45
, Chap. 3]
—  :

{\sf Sym}(H)\,:=\,\big\{\Psi\in{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})\colon\,\Psi(H)\subset H\big\},

where "
\subset
" is understood up to shrinking
H
,
and where the transformations
\Psi
are close to
the identity. Then
{\sf Sym}\,H
has Lie algebra:

{\sf Lie}\,{\sf Sym}(H)\,=\,\mathfrak{sym}(H)\,:=\,\big\{L\colon\,L\big|_{H}\,\,\text{tangent to}\,\,H\big\}.

Since all our considerations will be
local
,
we can assume that everything takes place in some
neighborhood of a fixed point
p_{0}\in H
.
Neighborhood shrinking is allowed
(a finite number of times).

Définition 1.2
.

The hypersurface
H
is said to be

(locally) affinely homogeneous
if:

T_{p_{0}}H\,=\,{\sf Span}_{\mathbb{R}}\,\big\{L\big|_{p_{0}}\colon\,L\in\mathfrak{sym}(H)\big\}.

According to Lie theory, the
1
-parameter groups

p\longmapsto{\sf exp}(t\,L)(p)
stabilize
H
, and

{\sf Sym}(H)
is then locally
transitive in a neighborhood of
p_{0}\in H
.

The problem of classifying all affinely homogeneous

n
-dimensional local analytic
smooth submanifolds
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+c}
is
probably of infinite complexity.
Even for
n=2=c
, it is not terminated.

In the hypersurface case
c=1

and in dimension
n=2
,
the classification
was terminated two decades ago
[
3
,
18
,
21
]
by Doubrov-Komrakov-Rabinovich,
by Eeastwood-Ezhov,

see
also
[
6
,
12
]
for
a differential invariants perspective.

In dimension
n=3
, and codimension
c=1
,
there is
[
15
]

in which all
multiply
transitive
models were classified, while
for the
special
affine subgroup

{\sf Saff}(\mathbb{R}^{3+1})\subset{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{R}^{3+1})
, there are
the (unpublished) Ph.D. thesis of
Marc Wermann
[
69
]
,
and the works of
Eastwood-Ezhov
[
24
,
27
]
,
both complete.

Joint with Chen
[
9
]
,
the author has studied the so-called
parabolic
surfaces

H^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, those whose Hessian has
constant rank
1

(
see
also
[
12
]
).
Somewhat analogous
5
-dimensional CR structures of dimension
5

whose Levi form is of constant rank
1
have
also been studied in
[
42
,
39
]
.

Problem 1.3
.

Study algebras of differential invariants
and classify homogenous models of
constant Hessian rank
1
hypersurfaces
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

A similar problem can be formulated in the context of
CR geometry,
cf.

[
54
]
.

In Winter 2021, using a computer, the author found
all affinely homogenous Hessian rank
1
hypersurfaces

H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
in dimensions
n=2
,
3
,
4
,

cf.

[
54
]
, forthcoming.

Then exploring dimensions
n=5,6,7
,
the author was surprised to realize
that there are
no
homogenous models,
except the degenerate ones obtained by taking a
product of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with a homogeneous hypersurface

H^{n-m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-m+1}
so that
2\leqslant n-m\leqslant 4
.

He then tackled to prove a
non-existence
result,
which, incidentally, provides a complete classification
(in the constant Hessian rank
1
branch).
But the computational task appeared to be unexpectedly hard,
and it took one year to write a detailed proof in general
dimension
n\geqslant 5
.

The main result of this paper concerns dimensions

n\geqslant 5
, but several results
are true for all
n\geqslant 2
,
and will be useful to
[
57
]
.

From the corpus of this article, we may emphasize three statements.
The first one appears as Theorem
13.1
below.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a local affinely homogeneous
hypersurface
having constant Hessian rank
1
. Then there exists
an integer
1\leqslant n_{H}\leqslant n
and
affine coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
in which:

H^{n}\,=\,H^{n_{H}}\times\mathbb{R}_{x_{n_{H}+1},\dots,x_{n}}^{n-n_{H}-1}

is a product of an affinely homogeneous
hypersurface
H^{n_{H}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{H}+1}

times a ‘dumb’
\mathbb{R}^{n-n_{H}-1}
,
and is graphed as:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\sum_{m=3}^{n_{H}}\,\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1}}(3)\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=n_{H}+2}^{\infty}\,E^{m}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{H}}),

with graphing function
F=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{H}})

independent of
x_{n_{H}+1},\dots,x_{n}
.

Notice that the variables
\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{H}},u\big)

are present
in such a graphed equation.

Of course, we are interested in the hypersurfaces for which
n_{H}=n
. Such hypersurfaces can be called
nondegenerate
,
but we will not use such a terminology.

With
n_{H}=n
, Theorem
1.4

shows the graphing function up to order
n+1
included.
Up to order
n+3
included, we prove

Theorem 1.5
.

In any dimension
n\geqslant 2
,
every local hypersurface
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
having constant Hessian rank
1
,
and which is not affinely equivalent to a product
of
\mathbb{R}^{m}

(1\leqslant m\leqslant n)

with a hypersurface
H^{n-m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-m+1}
,
can be affinely normalized up to order
n+3
as:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}}{2}+\sum_{m=3}^{n}\,\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{m}\,x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}\,x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}\Big)

\displaystyle+F_{n+1,10\cdots 0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}x_{2}}{(n+1)!}+x_{1}^{n}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=n+2}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}\,x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}

\displaystyle+F_{n+3,0\cdots 0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+3}}{(n+3)!}+F_{n+2,10\cdots 0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+2}\,x_{2}}{(n+2)!}+F_{n+2,0010\cdots 0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+2}\,x_{4}}{(n+2)!}+\cdots+F_{n+2,0\cdots 01}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+2}\,x_{n}}{(n+2)!}

\displaystyle+F_{n+1,10\cdots 0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}\,x_{2}\,x_{2}}{n!}+x_{1}^{n+1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=n+3}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}\,x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}

\displaystyle+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{n}}(3)+{\rm O}_{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}}(n+4).

Furthermore, linear
{\sf GL}(n+1,\mathbb{R})

self-maps (fixing the origin)

\big(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\big)=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)\,\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\big)

of such a hypersurface
are necessarily weigted dilations of the form:

y_{1}\,=\,{\text{c}}\,x_{1},\ \ \ \ \ \ y_{2}\,=\,0,\ \ \ \ \ \ y_{3}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{{\text{c}}}\,x_{3},\ \ \ \ \ \ \dots,\ \ \ \ \ \ y_{n}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{{\text{c}}^{n-2}}\,x_{n},\ \ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,{\text{c}}^{2}\,u,

with
{\text{c}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ast}
.

In other words, the isotropy is at most one-dimensional.
The more advanced Theorem
25.2

gives terms of orders
n+4
,
n+5
, which are more
complicated, but (unfortunately) necessary in order to establish
our (unexpected) main

Theorem 1.6
.

In any dimension
n\geqslant 5
,
there are no
affinely homogeneous constant Hessian rank 1
nondegenerate hypersurfaces
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Here is the key reason why homogeneous models do not exist
when
n\geqslant 5
.
In Sections
26

and
27
,
we will obtain the two equations

I
and
II

shown in Proposition
26.2
,
that will
contradict homogeneity, at the infinitesimal level.

Readers could admit
Theorem
1.5
or
Theorem
25.2
,
and go directly to
Sections
26

and
27
,
which are simple to read.
Unfortunately, the core normalizations
done in the previous sections are hard,
require patience, and indurance.

All computations were done on
a computer fully in dimensions
n=2,3,4,5,6,7
,
during
>
2 months of exploration,
from December 2020, to February 2021.
Especially, all technical statements of this article
were
constantly checked to be true
on a computer in
dimensions
n=5,6,7
. This acted as guide
to set up the general dimension by hand.

In fact, it happened to be unexpectedly hard to write
by hand a detailed
proof in general dimension  —  the
computer being unable to do that!
It was really necessary to normalize

\big\{u=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\big\}
up to order
n+5

as stated in Theorem
25.2
,
because no contradiction occured
in lower order
\leqslant n+4
for the
‘concrete’ dimensions
n=5,6,7
.

With slightly harder computations,
it can be shown that in any dimension
n\geqslant 2
:

{\sf dim}\,{\sf Lie}\,{\sf Sym}(H)\,\leqslant\,4,

always. Thus, when
{\sf dim}\,H=n\geqslant 5
,
(infinitesimal) homogeneity cannot take place.

All statements hold for
\mathbb{C}
instead of
\mathbb{R}
,
with the same proofs.

2
.
Hessian Matrix and its Rank Invariancy

We may assume that coordinates
(x,u)
are centered at
p_{0}\in M
, so
that
p_{0}
is the origin. Denote its image
by
q_{0}:=\Psi(p_{0})
. We may
also assume that
q_{0}
is
the origin in
\mathbb{R}_{y,v}^{n+1}
too.
Then
\Psi(0)=0
forces
0=\tau_{1}=\cdots=\tau_{n}=\tau_{0}

in (
1.1
), which
becomes a general
{\sf GL}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
transformation:

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\\
v\end{array}\right]\,=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}a_{1,1}&\cdots&a_{1,n}&b_{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{1}\\
c_{1}&\cdots&c_{n}&d\end{array}\right]\,\left[\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}\\
u\end{array}\right].

Importantly, the basic hypothesis that
\Psi(H)\subset K
, namely
that
0=-\,v+G(y)
when
\big(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\big)=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\big)

as written above
and when
u
is replaced by
F(x)
,
expresses as the
fundamental equation:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&\,=\,-\,c_{1}\,x_{1}-\cdots-c_{n}\,x_{n}-d\,F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\\
&\ \ \ \ \ +G\Big(a_{1,1}x_{1}+\cdots+a_{1,n}x_{n}+b_{1}F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),\,\dots\dots,\,a_{n,1}x_{1}+\cdots+a_{n,n}x_{n}+b_{n}F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\Big),\end{aligned}

(2.13)

which holds identically in
\mathbb{C}\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.

One step further, by composing
\Psi
with two elementary affine
transformations, we may assume that the tangent space
T_{0}H^{n}=\{u=0\}
is horizontal, and that
T_{0}K^{n}=\{v=0\}
as well. In
other words,
F={\rm O}_{x}(2)
and
G={\rm O}_{y}(2)
.

Thus, order
0
and order
1
terms are absent in the expansions:

u\,=\,F(x)\,=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1}\geqslant 0,\dots,\sigma_{n}\geqslant 0\atop\sigma_{1}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\geqslant 2}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}}{\sigma_{1}!\,\cdots\,\sigma_{n}!}\,F_{x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}}(0)\ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,G(y)\,=\,\sum_{\tau_{1}\geqslant 0,\dots,\tau_{n}\geqslant 0\atop\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{n}\geqslant 2}\,\frac{y_{1}^{\tau_{1}}\cdots y_{n}^{\tau_{n}}}{\tau_{1}!\,\cdots\,\tau_{n}!}\,G_{y_{1}^{\tau_{1}}\cdots y_{n}^{\tau_{n}}}(0).

Lemma 2.14
.

The linear transformation
\big(\begin{smallmatrix}y\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\big)=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\big)\big(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
u\end{smallmatrix}\big)
sends
u={\rm O}_{x}(2)

to
v={\rm O}_{y}(2)
if and only if
0=c_{1}=\cdots=c_{n}
.

Proof.

Read (
2.13
) modulo
{\rm O}_{x}(2)
:

0\,\equiv\,-\,c_{1}\,x_{1}-\cdots-c_{n}\,x_{n}-{\rm O}_{x}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(2).\qed

This interprets as
group reduction:

\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}a_{1,1}&\cdots&a_{1,n}&b_{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{n}\\
c_{1}&\cdots&c_{n}&d\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 0}}\,\,\,\leadsto\,\,\,\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}a_{1,1}&\cdots&a_{1,n}&b_{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{n}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&d\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 1}}.

Necessarily:

{\sf det}\,\big(a_{i,j}\big)\,\neq\,0\,\neq\,d.

Later, we will need an equivalent
expansion gathering homogeneous terms
of fixed order,
whose proof is left to the reader:

\displaystyle u\,=\,\sum_{m\geqslant 2}\,\tfrac{1}{m!}\,\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{n}\,\cdots\,\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{n}\,x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{m}}\,F_{x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{m}}}(0)\,\,=:\,\sum_{m\geqslant 2}\,F^{m}(x).

(2.15)

Next, at order
2
:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{n}\,\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{n}\,x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\,f_{i_{1},i_{2}}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{n}\,\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{n}\,y_{j_{1}}y_{j_{2}}\,g_{j_{1},j_{2}}+{\rm O}_{y}(3),

where:

f_{i_{1},i_{2}}\,:=\,F_{x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}}(0)\,=\,f_{i_{2},i_{1}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ g_{j_{1},j_{2}}\,:=\,G_{y_{j_{1}}y_{j_{2}}}(0)\,=\,g_{j_{2},j_{1}}.

With
x={}^{\text{\sc t}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
being a column vector,
we may abbreviate:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,{}^{\text{\sc t}}x\cdot F^{(2)}\cdot x+{\rm O}_{x}(3)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,{}^{\text{\sc t}}y\cdot G^{(2)}\cdot y+{\rm O}_{y}(3).

Définition 2.16
.

At any point
p\in H^{n}
, the

Hessian matrix
is defined, in any system
of coordinates
(x,u)
centered at
p=(0,0)

in which
H^{n}
is graphed as
u=F(x)
with

0=F(0)=F_{x_{1}}(0)=\cdots=F_{x_{n}}(0)
, as:

{\sf Hessian}(F)\,:=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)&\cdots&F_{x_{1}x_{n}}(0)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
F_{x_{n}x_{1}}(0)&\cdots&F_{x_{n}x_{n}}(0)\end{array}\right]\,=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}f_{1,1}&\cdots&f_{1,n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
f_{n,1}&\cdots&f_{n,n}\end{array}\right]\,=\,F^{(2)}.

At first sight, this definition depends on coordinates,
but a key invariancy lies behind.

Lemma 2.17
.

The rank of the Hessian matrix
{\sf Hessian}(F)
is independent of
the affine coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},u)

centered at
p

in which
0=F(0)=F_{x_{1}}(0)=\cdots=F_{x_{n}}(0)
.

Proof.

Take another system of coordinates

(y_{1},\dots,y_{n},v)
centered at
p=0
in which
the hypersurface is graphed as
v=G(y)
with

0=G(0)=G_{y_{1}}(0)=\cdots=G_{y_{n}}(0)
,
namely:

v\,=\,{}^{\text{\sc t}}y\cdot G^{(2)}\cdot y+{\rm O}_{y}(3).

So there is an invertible linear transformation
of the form (
2
)
with
0=c_{1}=\cdots=c_{n}
by Lemma
2.14
,
namely
y=A\cdot x+B\cdot u
and
v=d\,u
,
which sends
u=F(x)
to
v=G(y)
.

Then read the fundamental equation (
2.13
)
modulo
{\rm O}_{x}(3)
, compute,
observe that
{\rm O}_{y}(3)={\rm O}_{x}(3)
when
y=A\cdot x+B\cdot F(x)={\rm O}_{x}(1)
, factorize:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\,-\,v+G(y)

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,d\,F(x)+G\big(A\cdot x+B\cdot F(x)\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,d\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,{}^{\text{\sc t}}x\cdot F^{(2)}\cdot x-{\rm O}_{x}(3)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,{}^{\text{\sc t}}\big[A\cdot x+B\cdot F(x)\big]\cdot G^{(2)}\cdot\big[A\cdot x+B\cdot F(x)\big]+{\rm O}_{y}(3)

\displaystyle=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,{}^{\text{\sc t}}x\cdot\Big(-\,d\,F^{(2)}+{}^{\text{\sc t}}A\cdot G^{(2)}\cdot A\Big)\cdot x+{\rm O}_{x}(3),

and deduce, since
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is arbitrary, that:

d\,F^{(2)}\,=\,{}^{\text{\sc t}}A\cdot G^{(2)}\cdot A.

Lastly, from
d\neq 0\neq{\sf det}\,A
, conclude
that
{\sf rank}\,F^{(2)}={\sf rank}\,G^{(2)}
.
∎

Now, suppose that
H=\{u=F(x)\}
is given
in coordinates
(x,u)
centered at
p_{0}=0
,
with
0=F(0)=F_{x_{1}}(0)=\cdots=F_{x_{n}}(0)
.
At any other point
p\sim p_{0}
close to the origin
with
p=(x_{1,p},\dots,x_{n,p},u_{p})
, where

u_{p}=F(x_{p})
,
use the centered coordinates:

y_{1}\,:=\,x_{1}-x_{1,p},\,\,\dots\dots\,\,y_{n}\,:=\,x_{n}-x_{n,p},\,\,v\,:=\,u-F(x_{p})-F_{x_{1}}(x_{p})\big(x_{1}-x_{1,p}\big)-\cdots-F_{x_{n}}(x_{p})\big(x_{n}-x_{n,p}\big).

The new graphing function:

G(y)\,:=\,F(x_{p}+y)-F(x_{p})-F_{x_{1}}(x_{p})\,y_{1}-\cdots-F_{x_{n}}(x_{p})\,y_{n},

satisfies
G(0)=G_{y_{1}}(0)=\cdots=G_{y_{n}}(0)
,
hence Definition
2.16

applies.
But since the correction terms are of degree
\leqslant 1

in
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
, they disappear after second order
differentiation:

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}G_{y_{1}y_{1}}(0)&\cdots&G_{y_{1}y_{n}}(0)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
G_{y_{n}y_{1}}(0)&\cdots&G_{y_{n}y_{n}}(0)\end{array}\right]\,=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(x_{p})&\cdots&F_{x_{1}x_{n}}(x_{p})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
F_{x_{n}x_{1}}(x_{p})&\cdots&F_{x_{n}x_{n}}(x_{p})\end{array}\right].

Proposition-Définition 2.18
.

The Hessian of a graphed hypersurface
u=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

is a well defined matrix-valued function of
x
:

{\sf Hessian}(F)(x)\,:=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(x)&\cdots&F_{x_{1}x_{n}}(x)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
F_{x_{n}x_{1}}(x)&\cdots&F_{x_{n}x_{n}}(x)\end{array}\right]

whose rank is invariant under affine equivalences
(at pairs of points corresponding one to another).∎

3
.
Constant Hessian Rank
1

Take a hypersurface
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with

0\in H^{n}
and
T_{0}H^{n}=\{u=0\}
, graphed as:

u\,=\,F(x)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,F_{x_{i}x_{j}}(0)+{\rm O}_{x}(3).

Its Hessian at the origin
0
is represented by the

n\times n
matrix
\big(F_{x_{i}x_{j}}(0)\big)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Whenever
\big(F_{x_{i}x_{j}}(0)\big)
is not the zero matrix,
there exists an affine change of coordinates
y=A\,x
,

v=u
, making nonzero (after renaming
y=:x
):

F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)\,\neq\,0.

Proof.

If there exists an index
i_{\ast}
with
F_{x_{i_{\ast}}x_{i_{\ast}}}(0)\neq 0
, simply permute affine coordinates
to set
x_{1}:=x_{i_{\ast}}
.

Assume therefore that
0=F_{x_{i}x_{i}}(0)
for all

i=1,\dots,n
. By assumption, there exist

i_{\ast}
and
j_{\ast}\neq i_{\ast}
such that

F_{x_{i_{\ast}}x_{j_{\ast}}}(0)\neq 0
.
Rename
x_{1}:=x_{i_{\ast}}
to have
0\neq F_{x_{1}x_{j_{\ast}}}(0)
.
Change
j_{\ast}\geqslant 2
to be the smallest
satisfying
F_{x_{1}x_{j_{\ast}}}(0)\neq 0
.
Also, abbreviate
f_{i,j}:=F_{x_{i}x_{j}}(0)
.
So
f_{1,j_{\ast}}\neq 0
.

Since diagonal terms are all zero, only
\sum_{i<j}
remains,
and
we may expand,
modulo
{\rm O}_{x}(3)
:

\displaystyle u\,\equiv\,\sum_{i<j}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{i<j\atop j\leqslant j_{\ast}}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}+\sum_{i<j\atop j_{\ast}<j}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\,x_{1}x_{j_{\ast}}\,f_{1,j_{\ast}}+\sum_{j_{\ast}<j}\,x_{1}x_{j}\,f_{1,j}+\sum_{2\leqslant i<j\atop j\leqslant j_{\ast}}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}+\sum_{2\leqslant i<j\atop j_{\ast}<j}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}.

Now, set
x_{j_{\ast}}:=y_{1}+y_{j_{\ast}}
while

x_{j}:=y_{j}
for
j\neq j_{\ast}
and
u:=v
,
whence
{\rm O}_{x}(3)={\rm O}_{y}(3)
,
so that the first monomial becomes:

y_{1}\,\big(y_{1}+y_{j_{\ast}}\big)\,f_{1,j_{\ast}},

with nonzero coefficient for
y_{1}y_{1}
.
The three remaining sums cannot
incorporate
y_{1}y_{1}
. Thus, the new graph
v=G(y)

satisfies
G_{y_{1}y_{1}}(0)=2\,f_{1,j_{\ast}}\neq 0
.
∎

4
.
Independent and Border-Dependent Jets

Up to the end of the article, we will assume:

F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)\,\neq\,0.

Also, our main hypothesis will be that
the Hessian matrix
\big(F_{x_{i}x_{j}}(x)\big)
has
constant rank
1
for all
x\sim 0
in some neighborhood
of the origin.

It is elementary to verify that, for constants

\varphi_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\varphi_{1,1}\neq 0
:

1\,=\,{\sf rank}\,\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\varphi_{1,1}&\varphi_{1,2}&\cdots&\varphi_{1,n}\\
\varphi_{2,1}&\varphi_{2,2}&\cdots&\varphi_{2,n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\varphi_{n,1}&\varphi_{n,2}&\cdots&\varphi_{n,n}\end{array}\right]\ \ \ \ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ 0\,=\,\left|\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi_{1,1}&\varphi_{1,j}\\
\varphi_{i,1}&\varphi_{i,j}\end{array}\right|\ \ \ \ \ \forall\,\,2\,\leqslant\,i,j\,\leqslant\,n.

Main Hypothesis 4.1
.

For all
x\sim 0

in some neighborhood of the origin:

\displaystyle F_{x_{i}x_{j}}(x)\,\equiv\,\frac{F_{x_{1}x_{i}}(x)\,F_{x_{1}x_{j}}(x)}{F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(x)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall\,\,i,j=2,\dots,n.

(4.2)

By differentiating this identity with respect to
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
, it is not difficult to prove by induction that
every derivative
F_{x_{1}^{\tau}x_{2}^{i_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{i_{n}}}(x)

with
i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}\geqslant 2

and
\tau\in\mathbb{N}
arbitrary,
expresses as a polynomial
in the derivatives

F_{x_{1}^{i_{1}^{\prime}}x_{2}^{i_{2}^{\prime}}\cdots x_{n}^{i_{n}^{\prime}}}(x)

with
i_{1}^{\prime}\leqslant 1
, divided by
a certain power
\big(F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(x)\big)^{\ast}
.
We will in fact need to know
what formulas hold only for
i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}=2
.

Terminology 4.3
.

\bullet

The
independent jets
are the derivatives with
i_{1}=0

or
i_{1}=1
:

F_{x_{2}^{i_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{i_{n}}}(x),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ F_{x_{1}x_{2}^{i_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{i_{n}}}(x).

\bullet

The
border-dependent jets
are the derivatives:

F_{x_{1}^{\tau}x_{i}x_{j}}(x),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \tau\,\geqslant\,0,\ \ \ \ \ i,j=2,\dots,n.

The way how these
border-dependent jets
F_{x_{1}^{\tau}x_{i}x_{j}}
express in terms
of the independent jets can be seen by just
differentiating (
4.2
)
\nu
times
with respect to
x_{1}
.

Later, the following abbreviations will be used:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\,(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}),

\displaystyle f_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}

\displaystyle:=\,F_{x_{1}^{i_{1}}x_{2}^{i_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{i_{n}}}(0).

5
.
Normalization at Order
2

Thus with
f_{1,1}\neq 0
, start from:

u\,=\,F(x)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\,\sum_{j}\,f_{i,j}\,x_{i}x_{j}+{\rm O}_{x}(3).

Changing
u
to
-u
if needed,
we can assume
f_{1,1}>0
.
The plain dilation
y_{1}:=\sqrt{f_{1,1}}\,x_{1}

makes
f_{1,1}=1
,
namely, using again the letters
x
,
u
:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\underset{2\leqslant j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,x_{1}x_{j}\,f_{1,j}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\underset{2\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}+{\rm O}_{x}(3).

Remind that our Main Hypothesis
4.1

is (implicitly) assumed in all statements.

Assertion 5.1
.

There exists an affine change of coordinates which normalizes

u=F(x)
to:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3).

Proof.

First, absorb all monomials incorporating
x_{1}
:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\Big(\underbrace{x_{1}+\underset{2\leqslant j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,x_{j}\,f_{1,j}}_{=:\,\,\text{\tiny\rm new}\,\,x_{1}}\Big)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\Big(\underset{2\leqslant j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,x_{j}\,f_{1,j}\Big)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\underset{2\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}+{\rm O}_{x}(3),

to get, with modified
f_{i,j}
:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\underset{2\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,f_{i,j}+{\rm O}_{x}(3).

This means that:

0\,=\,F_{x_{1}x_{j}}(0)

[formula]

Lastly, (
4.2
) yields:

F_{x_{i}x_{j}}(0)\,=\,0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\scriptstyle{(\forall\,\,2\,\leqslant\,i,\,j\,\leqslant\,n)}}.\qed

Next, starting from
u=\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)
, the
goal is to normalize order
3
terms. But before increasing the order,
we must
conserve
or
stabilize
the normalization at order

2
. This conducts to
group reduction
:

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf GL}(n+1,\mathbb{R})}{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}a_{1,1}&a_{1,2}&\cdots&a_{1,n}&b_{1}\\
a_{2,1}&a_{2,2}&\cdots&a_{2,n}&b_{2}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&a_{n,2}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{n}\\
c_{1}&c_{2}&\cdots&c_{n}&d\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 0}}}\,\,\,\leadsto\,\,\,\overset{G_{\sf stab}^{1}}{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}a_{1,1}&a_{1,2}&\cdots&a_{1,n}&b_{1}\\
a_{2,1}&a_{2,2}&\cdots&a_{2,n}&b_{2}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&a_{n,2}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{n}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&d\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 1}}}\,\,\,\leadsto\,\,\,\overset{G_{\sf stab}^{2}}{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}a_{1,1}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&b_{1}\\
a_{2,1}&a_{2,2}&\cdots&a_{2,n}&b_{2}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&a_{n,2}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{n}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}a_{1,1}^{2}}\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2}}}.

Lemma 5.2
.

The subgroup
G_{\sf stab}^{2}
of
G_{\sf stab}^{1}
which
sends
u=\frac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)

to
v=\frac{1}{2}\,y_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{y}(3)

consists of matrices in
G_{\sf stab}^{1}
with:

0\,=\,a_{1,2}\,=\,\cdots\,=\,a_{1,n}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ d\,=\,a_{1,1}^{2}.

Proof.

Read (
2.13
) modulo
{\rm O}_{x}(3)

and annihilate the coefficients
of
x_{1}x_{2}
, …,
x_{1}x_{n}
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\,=\ -\,v+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{y}(3)

\displaystyle\,=\,-\,d\,u+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(a_{1,1}\,x_{1}+a_{1,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+a_{1,n}\,x_{n}+b_{1}\,u\big)^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)

\displaystyle\,\equiv\,-\,d\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}-{\rm O}_{x}(3)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,a_{1,1}^{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+a_{1,1}\,x_{1}\,\big(a_{1,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+a_{1,n}\,x_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(2)\big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\big(a_{1,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+a_{1,n}\,x_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(2)\big)^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3).\qed

6
.
Normalization at Order
3

Now, let order
3
terms appear:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\geqslant 0\atop\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{3}=3}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}x_{3}^{\sigma_{3}}}{\sigma_{1}!\,\sigma_{2}!\,\sigma_{3}!}\,f_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}+{\rm O}_{x}(4).

In the sum,
pick the monomial
\frac{1}{6}\,x_{1}^{3}\,f_{1,1,1}
.
Recall
x^{\prime}=(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
.
The remaining cubic terms
are of the form
x_{1}^{2}A(x^{\prime})+x_{1}B(x^{\prime})+C(x^{\prime})
.
So they are
{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)
. Since they are cubic,
they are of the product form
{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)
.
Thus:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,F(x)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\,x_{1}^{3}\,f_{1,1,1}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(4),

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\,G(y)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,y_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\,y_{1}^{3}\,g_{1,1,1}+{\rm O}_{y^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{y}(2)+{\rm O}_{y}(4),

Assertion 6.1
.

One can normalize
g_{1,1,1}:=0
.

Proof.

With free
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, use the map belonging to
G_{\sf stab}^{2}
:

y_{1}:=x_{1}+b_{1}\,u,\ \ \ \ \ y_{2}:=x_{2},\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ y_{n}:=x_{n},\ \ \ \ \ v:=u.

So
y^{\prime}=x^{\prime}
, hence
{\rm O}_{y^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{y}(2)={\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)
, and the fundamental
equation (
2.13
) reads:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{6}\,f_{1,1,1}\,x_{1}^{3}-{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)-{\rm O}_{x}(4)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(x_{1}+b_{1}\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)\big)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\,g_{1,1,1}\,\big(x_{1}+{\rm O}_{x}(2)\big)^{3}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(4)

\displaystyle\equiv\,\tfrac{1}{6}\,x_{1}^{3}\,\big[-f_{1,1,1}+3\,b_{1}+g_{1,1,1}\big]+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(4).

No monomial
x_{1}^{3}
can appear in remainders. Hence the coefficient
of
x_{1}^{3}
must vanish. This means that
g_{1,1,1}:=f_{1,1,1}-3\,b_{1}
necessarily. But since
b_{1}
is a free
parameter in the affine transformation,
we can choose
b_{1}:=\frac{1}{3}\,f_{1,1,1}
to normalize
g_{1,1,1}:=0
.
∎

To normalize further, we can restart from this
v=G(y)

having
g_{1,1,1}=0
, call it
u=F(x)
with
f_{1,1,1}=0
,
and again normalize the new target
v=G(y)
with

g_{1,1,1}=0
. In other words,
both hypersurfaces are normalized similarly (as always):

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,F(x)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+0+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(4),

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\,G(y)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,y_{1}^{2}+0+{\rm O}_{y^{\prime}}(1)\,{\rm O}_{y}(2)+{\rm O}_{y}(4).

Furthermore, before taking account
of the normalizations
f_{1,1,1}:=0

and
g_{1,1,1}:=0
, remind that
the current stability group
G_{\sf stab}^{2}
is:

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}a_{1,1}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&b_{1}\\
a_{2,1}&a_{2,2}&\cdots&a_{2,n}&b_{2}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&a_{n,2}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{n}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}a_{1,1}^{2}}\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2}}.

This being a subgroup of
{\sf GL}(n+1,\mathbb{R})
, its
block-trigonal determinant
must be nonzero, whence:

a_{1,1}\,\neq\,0\,\neq\,\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}a_{2,2}&\cdots&a_{2,n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{n,2}&\cdots&a_{n,n}\end{array}\right|.

Next, let cubic terms of the form
x_{1}^{2}\,{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)
appear:

u\,=\,F(x)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+0+\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}\,\big(\varphi_{2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+\varphi_{n}\,x_{n}\big)+x_{1}\,{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(3)+{\rm O}_{x}(4).

Assertion 6.2
.

The remaining cubic terms
x_{1}\,{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(3)\equiv 0
are zero.

Proof.

It suffices to show:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\bf?}}{\,=\,}F_{x_{1}x_{i}x_{j}}(0)

\scriptstyle{(\forall\,\,2\,\leqslant\,i,\,j\,\leqslant\,n),}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\bf?}}{\,=\,}F_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}}(0)

\scriptstyle{(\forall\,\,2\,\leqslant\,i,\,j,\,k\,\leqslant\,n).}

By previous normalizations, we have
0=F_{x_{1}x_{i}}(0)

for all
i=2,\dots,n
.
Differentiating (
4.2
) with respect to
x_{1}
and
to
x_{k}
yields these vanishings:

\displaystyle F_{x_{1}x_{i}x_{j}}(0)

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{F_{x_{1}x_{1}x_{i}}(0)\,\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{j}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}+\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{i}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}\,F_{x_{1}x_{1}x_{j}}(0)}{F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)}-\frac{\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{i}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}\,\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{j}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}\,F_{x_{1}x_{1}x_{1}}(0)}{F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)\,F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)}\,=\,0,

\displaystyle F_{x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}}(0)

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{F_{x_{1}x_{i}x_{k}}(0)\,\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{j}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}+\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{i}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}\,F_{x_{1}x_{j}x_{k}}(0)}{F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)}-\frac{\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{i}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}\,\underline{F_{x_{1}x_{j}}(0)}_{{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\circ}}\,F_{x_{1}x_{1}x_{k}}(0)}{F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)\,F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(0)}\,=\,0.\qed

Thus, the two hypersurfaces are:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,F(x)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+0+\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}\,\big(\varphi_{2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+\varphi_{n}\,x_{n}\big)+{\rm O}_{x}(4).

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\,G(y)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,y_{1}^{2}+0+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y_{1}^{2}\,\big(\psi_{2}\,y_{2}+\cdots+\psi_{n}\,y_{n}\big)+{\rm O}_{y}(4).

Assertion 6.3
.

The property
0=\varphi_{2}=\cdots=\varphi_{n}

is equivalent to
0=\psi_{2}=\cdots=\psi_{n}
.

Proof.

The general map of
G_{\sf stab}^{2}

which stabilizes the normalization up to order
2

writes:

\displaystyle y_{1}

\displaystyle=\,a_{1,1}\,x_{1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \,+b_{1}\,u,

\displaystyle y_{2}

\displaystyle=\,a_{2,1}\,x_{1}+a_{2,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+a_{2,n}\,x_{n}+b_{2}\,u,

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle y_{n}

\displaystyle=\,a_{n,1}\,x_{1}+a_{n,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+a_{n,n}\,x_{n}+b_{n}\,u,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ a_{1,1}^{2}\,u.

Therefore, the fundamental equation (
2.13
) writes:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,a_{1,1}^{2}\,\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}\,\big(\varphi_{2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+\varphi_{n}\,x_{n}\big)+{\rm O}_{x}(4)\Big]

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(a_{1,1}\,x_{1}+b_{1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)\big)^{2}

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(a_{1,1}\,x_{1}+{\rm O}_{x}(2)\big)^{2}\,\Big[\underset{2\leqslant j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,\psi_{j}\,\big(a_{j,1}\,x_{1}+a_{j,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+a_{j,n}\,x_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(2)\big)\Big]+{\rm O}_{x}(4).

Of course, the coefficient of
x_{1}^{2}
is zero.
Next, picking the coefficients, of
x_{1}^{3}

and of
x_{1}^{2}x_{2}
, …,
x_{1}^{2}x_{n}
, we get:

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle\,=\,-\,a_{1,1}\,\underset{2\leqslant j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,\psi_{j}\,a_{j,1},

(6.4)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\,=\,\underset{2\leqslant j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,\psi_{j}\,a_{j,2},\,\,\dots\dots\dots,\,\,\varphi_{n}\,=\,\underset{2\leqslant j\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,\psi_{j}\,a_{j,n}.

With the invertible
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix

(a_{j,k})
, we thus have
\varphi={}^{\text{\sc t}}a\cdot\psi
,
hence
\varphi=0
iff
\psi=0
.
∎

Before we discuss the two distinct affinely invariant cases

\varphi=0
and
\varphi\neq 0
, we must examine
infinitesimal affine automorphisms.

7
.
Tangency at Order
2

Take a hypersurface normalized at order
2
:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)\,\,=\,F(x).

A general affine vector field:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\,\,\,\,\,\,\Big(T_{1}+A_{1,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{1,n}\,x_{n}+B_{1}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\,+

\displaystyle+\Big(T_{2}+A_{2,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{2,n}\,x_{n}+B_{2}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\,+

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\,+

\displaystyle+\Big(T_{n}+A_{n,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{n,n}\,x_{n}+B_{n}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\,+

\displaystyle+\Big(T_{0}+C_{1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+C_{n}\,x_{n}+D\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u},

is tangent to the hypersurface
\{u=F(x)\}

if and only if:

\displaystyle L\big(-\,u+F(x)\big)\Big|_{u:=F(x)}\,\equiv\,0,

(7.1)

identically in
\mathbb{C}\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.

Here, neglecting terms of order
\geqslant 2
, this equation gives:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,T_{0}-C_{1}\,x_{1}-C_{2}\,x_{2}-\cdots-C_{n}\,x_{n}-D\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)

\displaystyle+\big(T_{1}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big)\,\big(x_{1}+{\rm O}_{x}(2)\big)

\displaystyle+\big(T_{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle+\big(T_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big)\,{\rm O}_{x}(2),

whence:

0\,=\,T_{0},\ \ \ \ \ C_{1}\,=\,T_{1},\ \ \ \ \ C_{2}\,=\,0,\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ C_{n}\,=\,0.

Thus:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\,=\,\,\,\,\,\,\Big(T_{1}+A_{1,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{1,n}\,x_{n}+B_{1}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\,+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\Big(T_{2}+A_{2,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{2,n}\,x_{n}+B_{2}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\,+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\,+

(7.2)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\Big(T_{n}+A_{n,1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+A_{n,n}\,x_{n}+B_{n}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\,+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\Big(\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ T_{1}\,x_{1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +D\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}.

8
.
The Product Case
H^{n}\cong H^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}

First, examine the
affinely invariant
case
\varphi_{2}=\cdots=\varphi_{n}=0
:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+0+{\rm O}_{x}(4).

Notice then that (
6.4
) becomes
b_{1}=0
.

Proposition 8.1
.

If such a hypersurface is affinely homogeneous, then

F(x)=F(x_{1})
is independent of
x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
.

Proof.

Now, examine the tangency equation (
7.1
)
using
L
from (
7
) modulo
{\rm O}_{x}(3)
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,T_{1}\,x_{1}-D\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(4)\big)

\displaystyle+\Big(T_{1}+A_{1,1}\,x_{1}+A_{1,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+A_{1,n}\,x_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(2)\Big)\,\big(x_{1}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)\big)

\displaystyle+\Big(T_{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\Big)\,{\rm O}_{x}(3)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle+\Big(T_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\Big)\,{\rm O}_{x}(3).

The coefficients of
x_{1}^{2}
, of
x_{1}x_{2}
, …, of
x_{1}x_{n}

must vanish, which gives:

D\,=\,2\,A_{1,1},\ \ \ \ \ A_{1,2}\,=\,0,\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ A_{1,n}\,=\,0.

Thus:

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle\,=\,\,\,\,\,\,\Big(T_{1}+A_{1,1}\,x_{1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +B_{1}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\,+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\Big(T_{2}+A_{2,1}\,x_{1}+A_{2,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+A_{2,n}\,x_{n}+B_{2}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\,+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\,+

(8.2)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\Big(T_{n}+A_{n,1}\,x_{1}+A_{n,2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+A_{n,n}\,x_{n}+B_{n}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\,+

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\Big(\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ T_{1}\,x_{1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +2\,A_{1,1}\,u\Big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}.

Next, let order
4
terms appear:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+0+F^{4}(x)+{\rm O}_{x}(5)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{where}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ F^{4}(x)\,:=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\geqslant 0\atop\sigma_{1}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=4}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}}{\sigma_{1}!\,\cdots\,\sigma_{n}!}\,f_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}}.

Assertion 8.3
.

This
F^{4}(x)=F^{4}(x_{1})
is independent of

(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
.

Proof.

Write
/
examine the tangency equation up to order
3
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,T_{1}\,x_{1}-2\,A_{1,1}\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(4)\big)

\displaystyle+\Big(T_{1}+A_{1,1}\,x_{1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +B_{1}\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(4)\big)\Big)\,\big(x_{1}+F_{x_{1}}^{4}+{\rm O}_{x}(4)\big)

\displaystyle+\big(T_{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big)\,\big(F_{x_{2}}^{4}+{\rm O}_{x}(4)\big)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle+\big(T_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big)\,\big(F_{x_{n}}^{4}+{\rm O}_{x}(4)\big).

This equation, viewed modulo
{\rm O}_{x}(4)
,
is a polynomial in
(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

of degree
\leqslant 3
which must vanish identically:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\,\equiv\,\ \ \ T_{1}\,F_{x_{1}}^{4}+B_{1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{3}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +T_{2}\,F_{x_{2}}^{4}

(8.4)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +T_{n}\,F_{x_{n}}^{4}.

Notation 8.5
.

[Coefficient picking]

For
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
given a converging power series:

E(x)\,=\,E(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\,=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}\geqslant 0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}}{\sigma_{1}!\,\sigma_{2}!\,\cdots\,\sigma_{n}!}\,E_{x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}}(0),

denote:

\big[x_{1}^{\tau_{1}}x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(E(x)\big)\,:=\,\frac{1}{\tau_{1}!\,\tau_{2}!\,\cdots\,\tau_{n}!}\,E_{x_{1}^{\tau_{1}}x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}}(0).

Therefore, in (
8
),
the coefficients of
all

monomials
x_{1}^{\tau_{1}}x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}

with
\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\cdots+\tau_{n}=3

must vanish.

Disregarding the monomial
x_{1}^{3}
in order not
to let
B_{1}
intervene,
i.e.

taking all

\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\cdots+\tau_{n}=3

with
\tau_{1}\neq 3
, we get:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\,\ \ \ T_{1}\,\Big(\big[x_{1}^{\tau_{1}}x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{1}}^{4}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+T_{2}\,\Big(\big[x_{1}^{\tau_{1}}x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{2}}^{4}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdot

\displaystyle+T_{n}\,\Big(\big[x_{1}^{\tau_{1}}x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{n}}^{4}\big)\Big).

The following goes almost without saying.

Observation 8.6
.

[Transitivity]

Since
T_{0}=0
, at the origin
0=(0,0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}_{x,u}^{n+1}
, the value of
L
is:

L\big|_{0}\,=\,T_{1}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\Big|_{0}+T_{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\Big|_{0}+\cdots+T_{n}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\Big|_{0},

and since also:

T_{0}H^{n}\,=\,{\sf Span}\,\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\Big|_{0},\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}\Big|_{0},\,\dots,\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\Big|_{0}\Big),

no linear relation between
T_{1},T_{2},\dots,T_{n}
only
can hold for
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to be affinely homogeneous.∎

Consequently, the coefficients of
T_{1}
, of
T_{2}
, …,
of
T_{n}
above must all vanish.
Restricting attention to
\tau_{1}=0
,
and taking all
(\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}

with
\tau_{2}+\cdots+\tau_{n}=3
,
we obtain:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\,\ \ \ T_{1}\,\Big(\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{1}}^{4}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+T_{2}\,\Big(\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{2}}^{4}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle+T_{n}\,\Big(\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{n}}^{4}\big)\Big),

whence:

0\,=\,\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{2}}^{4}\big),\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ \big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{n}}^{4}\big),

which is equivalent to:

0\,\equiv\,F_{x_{2}}^{4},\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ 0\,\equiv\,F_{x_{n}}^{4}.

Thus
F^{4}(x)
is independent of
x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
,
and can be denoted
E^{4}(x_{1})
.
∎

We can therefore let homogeneous terms of order
5
appear:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+0+E^{4}(x_{1})+F^{5}(x)+{\rm O}_{x}(6).

Directly, let us reason by induction. With
m\geqslant 5
,
assume:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+0+E^{4}(x_{1})+\cdots+E^{m-1}(x_{1})+F^{m}(x)+{\rm O}_{x}(m+1).

To terminate the proof of the proposition, we must show that

F^{m}(x)\equiv E^{m}(x_{1})
is independent of
x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
.

Using
L
from (
8
), write
/
examine
tangency equations up to order
m-1
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,-\,T_{1}\,x_{1}-2\,A_{1,1}\,\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+E^{4}+\cdots+E^{m-1}+{\rm O}_{x}(m)\Big)

\displaystyle+\Big[T_{1}+A_{1,1}\,x_{1}+B_{1}\,\big(\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+E^{4}+\cdots+E^{m-2}+{\rm O}_{x}(m-1)\big)\Big]\,\Big[x_{1}+E_{x_{1}}^{4}+\cdots+E_{x_{1}}^{m-1}+F_{x_{1}}^{m}+{\rm O}_{x}(m)\Big]

\displaystyle+\big[T_{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big]\,\big[F_{x_{2}}^{m}+{\rm O}_{x}(m)\big]

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdot

\displaystyle+\big[T_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big]\,\big[F_{x_{n}}^{m}+{\rm O}_{x}(m)\big].

In the first two lines, all appearing monomials
x_{1}^{\tau_{1}}x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}
with
\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}+\cdots+\tau_{n}\leqslant m-1
are such that
\tau_{1}\geqslant 1
, except the
ones in
T_{1}\,F_{x_{1}}^{m}
.

Therefore, when applying the coefficients-taking operators

[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}]({}_{{}^{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet\!}}}})
for all
(\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}

with
\tau_{2}+\cdots+\tau_{n}=m-1
, it remains only:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\,\ \ \ T_{1}\,\Big(\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{1}}^{m}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+T_{2}\,\Big(\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{2}}^{m}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle+T_{n}\,\Big(\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{n}}^{m}\big)\Big),

whence:

0\,=\,\big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{2}}^{m}\big),\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ \big[x_{2}^{\tau_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{n}}^{m}\big),

which is equivalent to:

0\,\equiv\,F_{x_{2}}^{m},\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ 0\,\equiv\,F_{x_{n}}^{m}.

Thus
F^{m}(x)
is independent of
x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
,
can be denoted
E^{m}(x_{1})
, and a plain induction
on
m\longrightarrow\infty
concludes
the proof of the proposition:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\sum_{m=4}^{\infty}\,E^{m}(x_{1})\,=:\,F(x_{1}).\qed

9
.
Interlude: Expansion of
F
in Homogeneous
(In)Dependent Monomials

Expand:

F(x)\,=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\geqslant 0}\,x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}\,F_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}},

with
F_{\sigma}:=\frac{1}{\sigma!}\,F_{x^{\sigma}}(0)
.
In accordance with
Terminology
4.3
,
let us introduce

Terminology 9.1
.

A monomial

x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}\,F_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}}
will be said:

\bullet

independent
if
\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\leqslant 1
;

\bullet

border-dependent
if

\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=2
;

\bullet

body-dependent
if

\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\geqslant 3
.

Then
F
decomposes into
3
parts:

F\,=\,\sum_{\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\leqslant 1}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}+\sum_{\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=2}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}+\sum_{\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\geqslant 3}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}.

If one wants to emphasize independent monomials only,
the dependent monomials can be gathered as a plain remainder:

F\,=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}\geqslant 0\atop\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\leqslant 1}\,x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}\,F_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{n}}(2).

If one wants to also show border-dependent monomials,
the body-dependent monomials can be gathered as a plain remainder:

F\,=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}\geqslant 0\atop\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\leqslant 1}\,x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}\,F_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}}+\sum_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}\geqslant 0\atop\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=2}\,x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}\,F_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{n}}(3).

The decomposition of
F(x)
as a sum of homogeneous terms
will be constantly used:

F(x)\,=\,\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\,\sum_{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=m}\,x_{1}^{\sigma_{1}}x_{2}^{\sigma_{2}}\cdots x_{n}^{\sigma_{n}}\,F_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}}\,\,=:\,\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\,F^{m}(x).

Then each
F^{m}(x)
can be subjected to similar decompositions:

\displaystyle F^{m}(x)

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=m\atop\ \ \ \sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\leqslant 1}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}+\sum_{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=m\atop\ \ \ \sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=2}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}+\sum_{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=m\atop\ \ \ \sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\geqslant 3}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=m\atop\ \ \ \sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\leqslant 1}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}+\sum_{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=m\atop\ \ \ \sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=2}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{n}}(3)

\displaystyle=\,\sum_{\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}=m\atop\ \ \ \sigma_{2}+\cdots+\sigma_{n}\leqslant 1}\,x^{\sigma}\,F_{\sigma}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{n}}(2).

Lastly, recall the abbreviation:

x^{\prime}\,:=\,(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}).

10
.
The Nondegenerate Order
3
Case and a Starting Induction

Disregarding the product case of
Section
8
,
assume that

(\varphi_{2},\dots,\varphi_{n})\neq(0,\dots,0)
.
A natural affine coordinate change:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}\,\big(\underbrace{\varphi_{2}\,x_{2}+\cdots+\varphi_{n}\,x_{n}}_{=:\,\,{\sf new}\,\,x_{2}}\big)+{\rm O}_{x}(4),

enables to normalize:

u\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{1}^{2}\,x_{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(4).

Now, set up a general inductive reasoning. For some integer
\nu

with
3\leqslant\nu\leqslant n
, assume that, modulo dependent
monomials which, by
Section
9
,
can be all gathered as a remainder
{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)
,
the hypersurface equation
is of the form:

u\,=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu-1}x_{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+1).

Then let appear all independent monomials of order
\nu+1
:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu-1}x_{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)!}

\displaystyle+\varphi_{1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{1}}{\nu!}+\cdots+\varphi_{\nu-1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu-1}}{\nu!}+\varphi_{\nu}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}+\cdots+\varphi_{n}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{n}}{\nu!}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2).

Again, if
(\varphi_{\nu},\dots,\varphi_{n})\neq(0,\dots,0)
,
a natural affine coordinate change:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu-1}x_{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)!}

\displaystyle+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}}{\nu!}\,\Big(\underbrace{\varphi_{1}\,x_{1}+\cdots+\varphi_{\nu-1}\,x_{\nu-1}+\varphi_{\nu}\,x_{\nu}+\cdots+\varphi_{n}\,x_{n}}_{=:\,\,{\sf new}\,\,x_{\nu}}\Big)+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2),

leads to:

u\,=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu-1}x_{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)!}+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2),

and then inductively to:

u\,=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}+\cdots+\frac{x_{1}^{n}x_{n}}{n!}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(n+2),

These are the hypersurfaces we mainly want to study:
they involve all variables
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
.

But before going further, we must examine
/
study
the circumstance where
\varphi_{\nu}=\cdots=\varphi_{n}=0
:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu-1}x_{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)!}

\displaystyle+\varphi_{1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{1}}{\nu!}+\cdots+\varphi_{\nu-1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu-1}}{\nu!}+0+\cdots+0+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(2)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2).

This degenerate branch will again lead to a product situation.

Since the arguments in the next
Section
11

will again involve applying an affine vector field
L

to the equation
0=-u+F(x)
, and since
L
is a
first-order derivation,
we need to know the border-dependent monomials as well.
Recall that, by Section
9
,
body-dependent monomials that are
not border-dependent can be gathered as
{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(3)
.

To organize properly the thought, we
consider simultaneously the
two cases
{\bf 0}\cdot\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}
and

{\bf 1}\cdot\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}
,
by setting up an

Induction Hypothesis 10.1
.

For some integer
\nu
with
3\leqslant\nu\leqslant n
,
the hypersurface equation writes:

\displaystyle u\,=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=3}^{\nu-1}\,\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\,\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!\,(j-1)!}+R^{m+1}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1})\Big)

\displaystyle+\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
{\bf 1}\end{smallmatrix}\right\}\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}+x_{1}^{\nu-1}\,\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}+R^{\nu+1}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n})+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2),

where
R^{m+1}
is homogeneous of order
m+1

in
(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1})
,
and is of order
\geqslant 3
in
(x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1})
,
where the
\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=\Lambda_{j,i}^{\nu}
are unknown constants,
and where
R^{\nu+1}
is homogeneous of order
\nu+1

in
(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n})
,
and is of order
\geqslant 3
in
(x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n})
.

We therefore assume that up to
m=\nu-1
, the values
of the border-dependent jets have been computed,
as they appear within the large parentheses.
For
\nu=3
, the formula holds true with empty

\sum_{m=3}^{\nu-1}
.
To close the induction on
\nu
,
we must show that
R^{\nu+1}=R^{\nu+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1})

is independent of
x_{\nu},x_{\nu+1},\dots,x_{n}
,
and we must determine the values
of the
\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}
. In the nondegenerate
branch
{\bf 1}\cdot\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}

that we will treat later,
we will have to show that
\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=0

whenever
i+j\neq\nu+1
, and
that
\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=\frac{1}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}

when
i+j=\nu+1
.

Abbreviate the homogeneous terms of
F(x)
normalized
up to order
\leqslant\nu
, namely up to

m=\nu-1
in the sum, as:

u\,=\,N^{2}(x_{1})+N^{3}(x_{1},x_{2})+\cdots+N^{\nu}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu-1})+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+1),

where, for
3\leqslant m\leqslant\nu-1
:

N^{m+1}\,:=\,\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\,\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!\,(j-1)!}+R^{m+1}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1}),

and abbreviate also the full dependent
remainder homogeneous of order
\nu+1

after
\big\{\begin{smallmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
{\bf 1}\end{smallmatrix}\big\}\,\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}
as:

S^{\nu+1}\big(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n}\big)\,:=\,x_{1}^{\nu-1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}+R^{\nu+1}\big(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n}\big),

which is of order
\geqslant 2
in
(x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n})
, so that:

u\,=\,F(x)\,=\,N^{2}+\cdots+N^{\nu}+\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
{\bf 1}\end{smallmatrix}\right\}\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}+S^{\nu+1}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2).

Assertion 10.2
.

This function
S^{\nu+1}
is independent of
x_{\nu},x_{\nu+1},\dots,x_{n}
.

Proof.

For any two indices
\nu\leqslant k,\ell\leqslant n
,
we must have by our constant Hessian rank
1
hypothesis:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,F_{x_{1}x_{1}}(x)\cdot F_{x_{k}x_{\ell}}(x)-F_{x_{1}x_{k}}(x)\cdot F_{x_{1}x_{\ell}}(x)

\displaystyle\equiv\,\big(1+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\big)\cdot\Big(S_{x_{k}x_{\ell}}^{\nu+1}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\Big)-\Big(\big\{\begin{smallmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
{\bf 1}\end{smallmatrix}\big\}\,\tfrac{x_{1}^{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)!}\,\tfrac{\partial x_{\nu}}{\partial x_{k}}+S_{x_{1}x_{k}}^{\nu+1}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\Big)\cdot\Big(\big\{\begin{smallmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
{\bf 1}\end{smallmatrix}\big\}\,\tfrac{x_{1}^{\nu-1}}{(\nu-1)!}\,\tfrac{\partial x_{\nu}}{\partial x_{\ell}}+S_{x_{1}x_{\ell}}^{\nu+1}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\Big)

\displaystyle\equiv\,S_{x_{k}x_{\ell}}^{\nu+1}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)-{\rm O}_{x}(2\nu-2),

since
2\nu-2\geqslant\nu
as
\nu\geqslant 3
,
and this yields:

0\,\equiv\,S_{x_{k}x_{\ell}}^{\nu+1},

Since
S^{\nu+1}
is of order
\geqslant 2
in

(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
, this concludes.
∎

11
.
The Product Case
H^{n}\cong H^{\nu-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-\nu}

Here, we treat the degenerate branch

{\bf 0}\cdot\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}
.
Thus:

u\,=\,F(x)\,=\,N^{2}(x_{1})+\cdots+N^{\nu}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1})+{\bf 0}+S^{\nu+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1})+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2).

Notice that the term
S^{\nu+1}

(homogeneous of order
\nu+1
)
depends only on
x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1}
,
as does the preceding one
N^{\nu}
.

Proposition 11.1
.

If such a hypersurface is affinely homogeneous, then

F=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1})
is independent of
x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n}
.

Proof.

Let homogeneous terms of order
\nu+2
appear:

u\,=\,F\,=\,N^{2}+\cdots+N^{\nu}+S^{\nu+1}+F^{\nu+2}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1},x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n})+{\rm O}(\nu+3).

We claim that
F^{\nu+2}
is independent of
x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n}
.

Indeed, using
L
from (
8
), write
/
examine
tangency equations up to order
\nu+1
:

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\footnotesize\begin{aligned} 0&\,\equiv\,-\,T_{1}\,x_{1}-2\,A_{1,1}\,\Big(N^{2}+\cdots+N^{\nu}+S^{\nu+1}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2)\Big)\\
&+\Big[T_{1}+A_{1,1}x_{1}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +B_{1}\big(N^{2}+\cdots+N^{\nu}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+1)\big)\Big]\cdot\Big[N_{x_{1}}^{2}+\cdots+N_{x_{1}}^{\nu}+S_{x_{1}}^{\nu+1}+F_{x_{1}}^{\nu+2}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2)\Big]\\
&+\Big[T_{2}+A_{2,1}x_{1}+\cdots+A_{2,\nu}x_{\nu}+\cdots+A_{2,n}x_{n}+B_{2}\big(N^{2}+\cdots+N^{\nu-1}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\big)\Big]\cdot\Big[N_{x_{2}}^{3}+\cdots+N_{x_{2}}^{\nu}+S_{x_{2}}^{\nu+1}+F_{x_{2}}^{\nu+2}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2)\Big]\\
&+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdot\\
&+\Big[T_{\nu-1}+A_{\nu-1,1}x_{1}+\cdots+A_{\nu-1,\nu}x_{\nu}+\cdots+A_{\nu-1,n}x_{n}+B_{\nu-1}\big(N^{2}+{\rm O}_{x}(3)\big)\Big]\cdot\Big[N_{x_{\nu-1}}^{\nu}+S_{x_{\nu-1}}^{\nu+1}+F_{x_{\nu-1}}^{\nu+2}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2)\Big]\\
&+\Big[T_{\nu}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\Big]\cdot\Big[F_{x_{\nu}}^{\nu+2}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2)\Big]\\
&+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdot\\
&+\Big[T_{n}+{\rm O}_{x}(1)\Big]\cdot\Big[F_{x_{n}}^{\nu+2}+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2)\Big].\end{aligned}

For all
(\tau_{\nu},\dots,\tau_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n-\nu+1}
with
\tau_{\nu}+\cdots+\tau_{n}=\nu+1
,
to this equation,
apply the coefficients-picking operators

[x_{\nu}^{\tau_{\nu}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}]({}_{{}^{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet\!}}}})
.
Since
N^{2}
,
N^{3}
, …,
N^{\nu}
,

S^{\nu+1}
depend only on
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1})
,
we obtain:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,\ \ \ T_{1}\,\Big([x_{\nu}^{\tau_{\nu}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}]\big(F_{x_{1}}^{\nu+2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots

\displaystyle+T_{\nu-1}\,\Big([x_{\nu}^{\tau_{\nu}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}]\big(F_{x_{\nu-1}}^{\nu+2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+T_{\nu}\,\Big([x_{\nu}^{\tau_{\nu}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}]\big(F_{x_{\nu}}^{\nu+2}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdot

\displaystyle+T_{n}\,\Big([x_{\nu}^{\tau_{\nu}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}]\big(F_{x_{n}}^{\nu+2}\big)\Big),

whence:

0\,=\,\big[x_{\nu}^{\tau_{\nu}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{\nu}}^{\nu+2}\big),\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ \big[x_{\nu}^{\tau_{\nu}}\cdots x_{n}^{\tau_{n}}\big]\big(F_{x_{n}}^{\nu+2}\big),

which is equivalent to:

0\,\equiv\,F_{x_{\nu}}^{\nu+2},\ \ \ \ \ \dots\dots,\ \ \ \ \ 0\,\equiv\,F_{x_{n}}^{\nu+2}.

Thus
F^{\nu+2}
is independent of
x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n}
,
and can be denoted
E^{\nu+2}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1})
.

Next, let homogeneous terms of order
\nu+3
appear:

u\,=\,F\,=\,N^{2}+\cdots+N^{\nu}+S^{\nu+1}+E^{\nu+2}+F^{\nu+3}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1},x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n})+{\rm O}(\nu+4).

By writing the tangency equation up to order
\nu+2
,
we realize similarly that

F^{\nu+3}
is independent of
x_{\nu},\dots,x_{n}
.
Proceeding by induction, we conclude that:

u\,=\,F\,=\,N^{2}+\cdots+N^{\nu}+S^{\nu+1}+\sum_{m=\nu+2}^{\infty}\,E^{m}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\nu-1}).\qed

We now disregard this degenerate case.

12
.
The Nondegenerate Case
{\bf 1}\cdot\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}

At last, we can start to treat the most interesting branch

{\bf 1}\cdot\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}
. Thus,
as already stated by the Induction
Hypothesis
10.1
,
we start from:

\displaystyle u\,=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=3}^{\nu-1}\,\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\,\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!\,(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1}}(3)\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{x_{1}^{\nu}x_{\nu}}{\nu!}+x_{1}^{\nu-1}\,\sum_{2\leqslant i,j\leqslant\nu-1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,x_{i}x_{j}\,\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu-1}}(3)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu+2),

where we already know from Assertion
10.2
that

\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=0
when
i\geqslant\nu
or
j\geqslant\nu
,
and that the body-dependent remainder
R^{\nu+1}
is independent of

x_{\nu},x_{\nu+1},\dots,x_{n}
, hence is of order
\geqslant 3
in

x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu-1}
.

Here is the statement we promised.

Proposition 12.1
.

One has
\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=0
whenever
i+j\neq\nu+1
,
and
\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=\frac{1}{(i-1)!\,(j-1)!}

when
i+j=\nu+1
.

Proof.

Firstly:

F_{x_{1}x_{1}}\,=\,1+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu-1}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu).

Secondly, for any two indices
2\leqslant i,j\leqslant\nu-1
:

\displaystyle F_{x_{1}x_{i}}

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{i-1}}{(i-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu-1}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu),

\displaystyle F_{x_{1}x_{j}}

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu-1}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu).

Here, we can also write
{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)
instead
of the more precise (but not useful)

{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{\nu-1}}(1)
.

Thirdly, to compute
F_{x_{i}x_{j}}
, consider two subcases:

\bullet

when
4\leqslant i+j\leqslant\nu
:

F_{x_{i}x_{j}}\,=\,x_{1}^{i+j-2}\,\tfrac{1}{(i-1)!\,(j-1)!}+x_{1}^{\nu-1}\,\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu);

\bullet

when
\nu+1\leqslant i+j\leqslant 2\nu-2
:

F_{x_{i}x_{j}}\,=\,0+x_{1}^{\nu-1}\,\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu).

The Hessian vanishing writes:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv\,F_{x_{1}x_{1}}\cdot F_{x_{i}x_{j}}-F_{x_{1}x_{i}}\cdot F_{x_{1}x_{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv\,\Big(1+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\Big)\Big(\big\{\begin{smallmatrix}{\bf 0}\\
{\bf 1}\end{smallmatrix}\big\}\,\tfrac{1}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}\,x_{1}^{i+j-2}+x_{1}^{\nu-1}\,\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\Big)

\displaystyle-\Big(\tfrac{x_{1}^{i-1}}{(i-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\Big)\Big(\tfrac{x_{1}^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(1)+{\rm O}_{x}(\nu)\Big),

and it can be expanded in the various cases.

\bullet

When
4\leqslant i+j\leqslant\nu
, it gives

\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=0
.

\bullet

For
i+j=\nu+1
, it gives

\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=\frac{1}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}
.

\bullet

When
\nu+2\leqslant i+j\leqslant 2\nu-2
, it gives

\Lambda_{i,j}^{\nu}=0
.
∎

This closes induction from
\nu-1
to
\nu
,
while
3\leqslant\nu\leqslant n
,

cf.
Induction Hypothesis
10.1
.

13
.
Summary and Beyond

We started from any hypersurface
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

whose Hessian has constant rank
1
and we showed,
generally, that one can let appear
monomials
\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}
,

\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2!}
,
\frac{x_{1}^{3}x_{3}}{3!}
,

\frac{x_{1}^{4}x_{4}}{4!}
,
\frac{x_{1}^{5}x_{5}}{5!}
,
…, until the process stops, and
we proved the following

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
H^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be a local affinely homogeneous
hypersurface
having constant Hessian rank
1
. Then there exists
an integer
1\leqslant n_{H}\leqslant n
and
affine coordinates
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
in which:

H^{n}\,=\,H^{n_{H}}\times\mathbb{R}_{x_{n_{H}+1},\dots,x_{n}}^{n-n_{H}-1}

is a product of an affinely homogeneous
hypersurface
H^{n_{H}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n_{H}+1}

times a ‘dumb’
\mathbb{R}^{n-n_{H}-1}
,
and is graphed as:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\sum_{m=3}^{n_{H}}\,\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1}}(3)\Big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{m=n_{H}+2}^{\infty}\,E^{m}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{H}}),

with graphing function
F=F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n_{H}})

independent of
x_{n_{H}+1},\dots,x_{n}
.∎

Disregarding the product cases (branches)

n_{H}=1,\dots,n_{H}=n-1

which amount to similar
considerations in lower dimensions, we will from now on
study the class of
nondegenerate

hypersurfaces, those involving
all
variables

(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
:

u\,=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\sum_{m=3}^{n}\,\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1}}(3)\Big)+{\rm O}_{x}(n+2).

The next (substantial) task is to examine further the remainder

{\rm O}_{x}(n+2)
.

We gather all remainders
{\rm O}_{x_{2},\dots,x_{m-1}}(3)

plainly as
{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(3)
, where
x^{\prime}:=(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
,
and we let appear the independent homogeneous
monomials of order
n+2
, namely:

F_{n+2,0,\dots,0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}x_{1}}{(n+2)!}+F_{n+1,1,\dots,0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}x_{2}}{(n+1)!}+\cdots+F_{n+1,0,\dots,1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}x_{n}}{(n+1)!}.

A reasoning similar to that of the proof
of Proposition
12.1

shows that border-dependent monomials of homogeneous
order
n+2

have the same form, hence the equation of the
hypersurface writes:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}+\sum_{m=3}^{n}\,\Big(\frac{x_{1}^{m}x_{m}}{m!}+x_{1}^{m-1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}\Big)

\displaystyle+F_{n+2,0,\dots,0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}x_{1}}{(n+2)!}+F_{n+1,1,\dots,0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}x_{2}}{(n+1)!}+\cdots+F_{n+1,0,\dots,1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{n+1}x_{n}}{(n+1)!}

\displaystyle+x_{1}^{n}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=n+2}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{x^{\prime}}(3)+{\rm O}_{x}(n+3).

Question 13.2
.

How to normalize these order
n+2

independent coefficients

F_{n+2,0,\dots,0}
,
F_{n+1,1,\dots,0}
,
…,
F_{n+1,0,\dots,1}
?

14
.
Normalization of Order
n+2
Terms

Consider therefore
another
similar (nondegenerate) hypersurface,
in coordinates
(y_{1},\dots,y_{n},v)
:

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\,\frac{y_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{y_{1}^{2}y_{2}}{2}+\sum_{m=3}^{n}\,\Big(\frac{y_{1}^{m}y_{m}}{m!}+y_{1}^{m-1}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=m+1}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}\Big)

\displaystyle+G_{n+2,0,\dots,0}\,\frac{y_{1}^{n+1}y_{1}}{(n+2)!}+G_{n+1,1,\dots,0}\,\frac{y_{1}^{n+1}y_{2}}{(n+1)!}+\cdots+G_{n+1,0,\dots,1}\,\frac{y_{1}^{n+1}y_{n}}{(n+1)!}

\displaystyle+y_{1}^{n}\sum_{i,j\geqslant 2\atop i+j=n+2}\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{(i-1)!(j-1)!}+{\rm O}_{y^{\prime}}(3)+{\rm O}_{y}(n+3).

From Section
5
,
we know what is the stability subgroup at order
2
.

Step 1.

Determine subgroup reduction stabilizing
terms of order
\leqslant n+1
:

\overset{G_{\sf stab}^{2}}{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}a_{1,1}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&b_{1}\\
a_{2,1}&a_{2,2}&\cdots&a_{2,n}&b_{2}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n,1}&a_{n,2}&\cdots&a_{n,n}&b_{n}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}a_{1,1}^{2}}\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2}}}\,\,\,\leadsto\,\,\,\overset{G_{\sf stab}^{n+1}}{\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}?&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&?\\
?&?&\cdots&?&?\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
?&?&\cdots&?&?\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&\cdots&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}a_{1,1}^{n+1}}\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2}}}.

Step 2.

Determine how this subgroup acts on order
n+2
terms,
and normalize those coefficients among

G_{n+2,1,\dots,0}
,
G_{n+1,1,\dots,0}
, …,

G_{n+1,0,\dots,1}
which can be normalized.

This computational task being nontrivial,
let us start concretely, in low dimensions.
The calculations of the next Section will not be detailed,
and the remainders
{\rm O}_{x}(\ast)

will not be written.

15
.
Stabilizing Order
n+1
Terms
in Dimensions
n=2,3,4,5,6

\bullet

In dimension
n=2
:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+F_{4,0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{3}x_{1}}{24}+F_{3,1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{3}x_{2}}{6},

the stability group is:

G_{{\sf stab}}^{3}\colon\ \ \ \ \ \left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}a_{1,1}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}-a_{1,1}a_{2,1}}\\
a_{2,1}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}1}&b_{2}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}a_{1,1}^{2}}\end{array}\right]^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 3}},

and its action gives:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\overset{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}40}}{\,\,=\,\,}-\,\frac{1}{24}\,a_{1,1}^{2}\,F_{4,0}+\frac{1}{24}\,a_{1,1}^{4}\,G_{4,0}+\frac{1}{6}\,a_{1,1}^{3}\,a_{2,1}\,G_{3,1}+\frac{1}{8}\,a_{1,1}^{2}\,a_{2,1}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\,a_{1,1}^{2}\,\boxed{b_{2}},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\overset{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}31}}{\,\,=\,\,}-\,\frac{1}{6}\,a_{1,1}^{2}\,F_{3,1}+\frac{1}{6}\,a_{1,1}^{3}\,G_{3,1}.

The free group parameter
b_{2}
can be used
to normalize
G_{4,0}:=0
.

\bullet

In dimension
n=3
:

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\,\frac{x_{1}^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{x_{1}^{3}x_{3}}{6}+\frac{x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle+F_{5,0,0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{4}x_{1}}{120}+F_{4,1,0}\,\frac{x_{1}^{4}x_{2}}{24}+F_{4,0,1}\,\frac{x_{1}^{4}x_{3}}{24}+\frac{x_{1}^{3}x_{2}x_{3}}{2},

the stability group is:
